0, c1 for each ctx; at each bit, compute the split the same way, update the count for the
 chosen bit; maintain [lo, hi) ⊆ [frac, frac+range) on the current scale; when range < 255
, emit a byte.

  Strategy:

  2. Build a program: a greedy LZ with off ≤ Q, len ≥ 1, prioritizing copies (long matches), falling

  4. If > 2500, improve the program (e.g., a DP that optimizes the number of bits under

  But note: the arithmetic coder's efficiency depends on the context model's adaptation; the first
 use of each context has a +1 prior (a split with c0=c1=0: split = range * 1/2). The
 contexts are used sparsely (the ctx 0 W-bits are used for the j-mantissa; ctx 1 is per symbol;
 ctx 8 is per literal; ctx 198/203+ for copies; ctx 297/300+ for len; ctx 891/896+ for
 literals' mag). The model is a simple per-context bit bias, adapting with counts
 — it's like a "bit-level adaptive" coder: it can at best exploit correlations that the
 per-context counts capture (marginal bit biases). So the achievable size ≈ the sum over contexts
 of (number of bits at that context) * (redundancy based on the marginal bias). The coder cannot exploit
 cross-bit structure beyond what the contexts capture.

  So the program choice should match what the model can capture: e.g., a literal mag for text: B
-bits at ctx 891: their distribution depends on the character set. The coder will learn the marginal bias of each B-bit... but ctx 891 is the same context for all mag B-bits
! So all B bits of mags (across all literals, all positions) share one context. The bias learned will be the

  ```python
  # Simulate the decoder's contexts exactly
  class Coder:
      def __init__(self):
          self.counts = {}  # ctx -> [c0, c1]
          self.range = 1
          self.frac = 0     # the decoder's fraction — I track this to emit digits
          self.lo = 0
          self.hi = 1       # valid interval on the current scale (255^D)
          self.out = bytearray()

      def ctx(self, c):
          if c not in self.counts: self.counts[c] = [0, 0]
          return self.counts[c]

      def emit_digit(self):
          # Need to choose d such that [lo*255, hi*255) ⊆ [255*frac + d, 255*frac +
 d + 255*range)
          lo_bound = 255*(hi - self.frac - self.range)  # d >= this
          hi_bound = 255*(self.lo - self.frac)          # d <= this
          # clamp to 0..254
          d = max(0, lo_bound)
          if d > hi_bound or d > 254:
              # fallback: should not happen; try another
              ...
          self.out.append(d + 1)
          self.frac = 255*self.frac + d
          self.range = 255*self.range
          # shift the interval: it's on the new scale
          self.lo *= 255
          self.hi *= 255

      def get_bit_enc(self, ctx, bit):
          if self.range < 255:
              self.emit_digit()
          c = self.ctx(ctx)
          c0, c1 = c
          split = self.range * (c0 + 1) // (c0 + c1 + 2)
          # match the decoder's integer arithmetic exactly: the decoder does range * -
~*counts / (sum + 2)
          # all int; Python's // matches C's int division for non-negative values

          if bit == 0:
              self.hi = self.lo + split
          else:
              self.lo = self.lo + split
          c[bit] += 1
          # note: the decoder's range/fract update is mirrored only via the invariant; but
 I also need
          # to update frac/range to mirror the decoder! Wait...
  ```

's frac is determined. At a bit with context ctx, split = range(c0+1)//(c0+c1+2) (the
 counts are determined by the bit history = by the program + ... the counts depend
 only on the bits decoded, which are the program's bits — known to the encoder). I need:

  - If encoding bit 0: frac < split
  - If encoding bit 1: frac ≥ split
  And the resyncs: when range < 255: the decoder reads one digit d (which I emit): frac → 2
55·frac + d.

  The encoder must choose each d to satisfy the upcoming constraint. This is a standard "ar
ithmetic encoder with deferred digit decisions": maintain the set of possible frac values...
 but frac is a single concrete value determined by my digits. So I simulate it: frac
 is known. At a resync, choose d ∈ [0,254] such that: (a) frac' = 255·frac + d < range' =
 255·range (the decoder requires frac < range always — frac ∈ [0, range)), and (b
) the upcoming bit constraints can be satisfied: for the next bit b with split': if
 b=0: frac' < split'; if b=1: frac' ≥ split'. But there may be multiple resyncs before the next
 bit... no: the resync happens at the start of a get_bit call; there's at most one
 resync between two bits? get_bit: if range < 255: resync (one byte). Then the bit. So
 between two consecutive bits, at most one resync (since range ≥ 255 after the resync, and
 the bit divides the range by ≥ 2... range can still be ≥ 255 after the bit → no resync at
 the next bit. Or range after the resync = 255·(old range) ≥ 255, then the split:
 range = split or range - split: can it be < 255 again? 255·r, r ≥ 1: split ≈ 255
·r·p: if p is small, split can be < 255 → the next bit resyncs again. So consecutive resyncs
 are possible (when the probabilities are extreme: the counts accumulate, p → 1/(n
+2) small: split = 255r·1/(n+2)... after many 0s, c0 is large: split = range·(c0+1)/(
c0+c1+2) ≈ range → the bit-0 branch: range' = split ≈ range; the bit-1 branch: range' =
 range - split = range·(c1+1)/(c0+c1+2) small → the next resync. Yes, consecutive
 resyncs are possible.)

  So the encoder: at each resync, choose d ∈ [0, 254] with the constraint frac' = 2
55·frac + d < 255·range (the window), and such that the next bit constraint can be satisfied
:
  - Next bit 0, split' (computed with the current range' = 255·range): need frac' < split
' → 255·frac + d < split' → d < split' - 255·frac.
  - Next bit 1: need frac' ≥ split' → d ≥ split' - 255·frac.
  (If the next "bit" is itself inside another get_bit... no — bits are atomic; each get_bit is one resync (maybe) + one bit. The sequence of get
_bit calls is known in advance from the program! I know the entire bit sequence and context
 in advance. So: simulate: for each bit, at the resync (if range < 255), I need to pick
 d now. d affects frac, which affects this bit's feasibility and all future ones.


  The standard solution (and the reason for the "interval" approach): the set of valid d
's is an interval; if I choose d wisely, the set of "valid frac prefixes" stays consistent
. The clean, provably correct approach: maintain the interval [lo, hi) of frac values (
on the current scale) that are "still viable" — i.e., there exists a continuation of digits
 (including the current one, to be chosen) that satisfies all future constraints... that's
 getting into the weeds.

  A much simpler approach that's known to work: the "pick the midpoint and verify
" approach — but there's an even simpler, robust way: since I can re-run the real decoder
 to verify, I can use a practical algorithm:

  A practical encoder algorithm (greedy with lookahead):
  - Simulate the decoder's (frac, range, counts) exactly.
  - Precompute the entire bit sequence (from the program): a list of (ctx, bit).
  - Iterate: at each bit: if range < 255: I must emit a digit d. Choose d as follows: let
 (split, b) be this bit's parameters (the split is computed from the current counts, range
' = 255·range after the resync; note: the split uses the post-resync range: in get
_bit, the resync happens first, then split = range·(c0+1)/(c0+c1+2) with the new range).

    - Need: b=0: d < split - 255·frac; b=1: d ≥ split - 255·frac.
    - Also d ∈ [0, 254].
    - Choose d in the valid range that keeps frac' "central" in [0, 255·range): e
.g., the midpoint of the valid range, or d = split - 255·frac (for b=1, the tight lower bound
) or split - 255·frac - 1 (for b=0, the tight upper bound)... but the tight choice
 may break future constraints; the midpoint keeps the options open. Since the range
' = 255·range can be up to 255·254, and the split divides it, the midpoint heuristic
 is very safe. But "safe" is not proven — I'll verify with the real decoder. If it fails,
 I'll adjust (e.g., DP over d at resync points, or a "recenter" resync trick: when the interval
 [lo,hi) is entirely in the middle third, I can emit an extra digit without the decoder knowing — no
, I can't emit an extra byte; the decoder reads exactly one byte per resync).

  Actually, the cleanest and correct approach: maintain the feasible interval of frac. Define

  So feasibility depends on the frac history. The interval approach is necessary: maintain
 the set of (frac, range, counts) states... the counts/range are fixed by the bit

 The set of viable fracs: initially {0}... wait, the initial frac = 0, fixed. So frac

  But d_i also affects the next resync's frac, etc. So it's a chain of constraints on (
d_1, ..., d_n). A greedy "choose d to keep frac in the middle" is heuristic; a correct method:

  Let me define the sequence: resync points r_1..r_n (each followed by bit i_j). Forward

  So the encoder algorithm:
  1. From the program, build the bit list: [(ctx, bit), ...].
  2. Forward pass: compute the (range, counts) evolution — deterministic from the

  Now, the program-search problem. Let me define:
  - S = data.txt, N = 4868.
  - A program: a sequence of j symbols; each symbol: L(b) literal byte b, or C(off,
 len) (off ≤ the current position Q, len ≥ 1, S[Q:Q+len] = S[Q-off:Q-off+len] (with the overlap semantics: copy
 one byte at a time: out[Q+i] = out[Q-off+i]).
  - Constraint: the total output = exactly N. (The j symbols are free.)
  - Cost (approximate): the number of resyncs in the arithmetic stream of the bit program
.

  Bit program for a symbol:
  - Literal L(b): bit0: ctx 1, bit 0. bit1: sign: ctx 8, bit s (s = 1 if I choose the
 negative sign... (1-2*bit): bit=0 → +1, bit=1 → -1). mag = get_integer(4, 9): m =
 mag: k = 0 if m < 16, otherwise the smallest k such that m < 2^(4+k)... m ∈ [2^(
4+k-1), 2^(4+k)) → k. Bits: k zeros (ctx 896+k... wait the while contexts: tmp0=4,
 ctx_param=9 → base ctx = 4*... `ctx*=99`: ctx = 891. while: ++tmp + ctx: first tmp
 = 5 → 896. So the contexts: 896, 897, ..., 895+k. Then the 1-bit at ctx 895+k...
 wait: the while loop: tmp starts at 4 (the parameter). Iteration 1: ++tmp → 5, ctx arg
 = 5 + 891 = 896. Iteration 2: tmp=6 → 897. ... Iteration k+1 (the one that reads
 the 1): tmp = 4 + (k+1) → ctx = 895 + k + ... 4 + k + 1 + 891 = 896 + k. So the zeros are
 at ctx 896..895+k... the k zeros: ctx 896, 897, ..., 895+k; the 1 at ctx 896 + k.
 Then the W-loop: tmp after the loop = 4 + k + 1; tmp-- → 4 + k; the W-bits: (4+k) bits at
 ctx 891: B = m - (2^k - 1)·16, width 4+k.
    - Note: m = 0 → k = 0: the 1-bit at ctx 896, 4 B-bits at 891 (all 0).
    - The sign choice: for b ∈ [0,127]: m = b, sign + (bit 0 at ctx 8). For b ∈ [128,
 255]: m = 256 - b, sign - (bit 1 at ctx 8). (The alternative for b < 128: sign -, m = 2
56 + b → larger. I choose the minimum m.)
  - Copy C(off, len): bit at ctx 1: 1. off: get_integer(5, 2): o = off - 1: k(o): 0 if o < 32, otherwise the smallest k such that o < 2^(5+k):
 o ∈ [2^(5+k-1), 2^(5+k)). Zero contexts: base: tmp0=5, ctx_param=2 → ctx = 198. while: ++tmp
+ctx: first 6+198 = 204?? Wait: tmp starts at 5. Iteration 1: ++tmp → 6, arg = 6 +
 198 = 204. Hmm, earlier I said 203 — let me recompute: ctx_param*99 = 198. tmp0 = 5.
 First while bit: tmp = 6 → 6 + 198 = 204. So the contexts: 204, 205, ..., the 1 at 20
4 + k. W: (5+k) bits at ctx 198: B = o - (2^k - 1)·32.
  - len: get_integer(2, 3): l = len - 1: k: 0 if l < 4, otherwise l ∈ [2^(2+k-1), 
2^(2+k)). while ctx: tmp0 = 2, ctx_param = 3 → 297. First: 3 + 297 = 300. Contexts: 
300, 301, ..., the 1 at 300+k. W: (2+k) bits at ctx 297: B = l - (2^k - 1)·4.
  - j: get_integer(9, 0): J = j: k: 0 if J < 512, otherwise J ∈ [2^(9+k-1), 2^(9+k)).
 while ctx: ctx_param = 0 → 0. First: tmp=10 → 10. Contexts: 10, 11, ..., the 1 at
 10+k. W: (9+k) bits at ctx 0: B = J - (2^k - 1)·512.

  The raw bit count of a symbol: literal: 1 + 1 + (1 + k_m + 4 + k_m) = 7 + 2·k_m... wait
: the while: k zeros + 1 = k+1 bits; the W: 4 + k. Total mag bits = k + 1 + 4 + k =
 5 + 2k. Literal total: 1 (type) + 1 (sign) + 5 + 2k_m = 7 + 2k_m. m < 16 (k=0): 7 bits.
 m ∈ [16, 31]... for text bytes, m ≤ 127 mostly: m ≤ 127: k_m: m < 16: 0; [16,31]: 1
; [32,63]: 2; [64,127]: 3. So literals: 7, 9, 11, 13 bits.
  Copy: 1 + (5 + 2k_o) + (3 + 2k_l) = 9 + 2k_o + 2k_l. o < 32 (k=0), l < 4 (k=0): 9 bits
.

  The total raw bit count ≈ Σ. Then the arithmetic coder compresses based on the context
 marginal biases. The resync count = the size.

  This is a substantial combinatorial optimization. Let me simplify:

  Insight: the cost in resyncs ≈ the total entropy under the adaptive per-context model. A
 good program: use copies whenever there's a match (even short ones with a small off),
 and use literals otherwise. The j symbols: a mix.

  Also, I should consider that j (the number of symbols) is coded at the start: j = the
 total number of symbols, known only after the program is built. No problem.

  Let me think about the scale of the resync count: each resync occurs when the range
 drops below 255. The range evolution: at each bit, range is multiplied by p or (1-p
), where p = (c0+1)/(c0+c1+2) (the probability of 0). The resyncs are like "normal
ization events": roughly, the number of resyncs ≈ the number of bits / log2(255) ≈ bits/8
 · ... each resync consumes log2(255) ≈ 7.996 bits of "range precision". So bytes ≈ total_bits / 8 (asymptotically, ignoring
 the adaptive overhead). So size ≈ raw bit count / 8 + a little. Raw bit count for all-literals
: 4868 symbols × (7 + 2k_m) + the j code (j = 4868: k_J: 4868 ∈ [2^12=4096, 2^13=819
2) → k=3 (2^(9+k) > 4868: 2^12 → k=3: [2^12, 2^13): J ∈ [2^(9+3-1)=4096, 8192): yes
 k=3) → j code: 1 + 3 + 12 = 16 bits. Total ≈ 4868·(average 7 + 2·k_m) + 16. The average k_m
 for text: the byte distribution: for printable text, m: most bytes in [32,126] → k=
2 (32-63) or 3 (64-127): roughly half each, plus some k=0 (space? space = 32 → m=
32 → k=2; hmm m=0..15: which bytes give m < 16? b ∈ [0,15] or b ∈ [241,255] — rare in text
). So the average k_m ≈ 2.7 → average literal bits ≈ 12.4 → total ≈ 4868·12.4 + 1
6 ≈ 60,400 bits ≈ 7550 bytes. Way over 2500.

  With copies: each copy replaces len literal bytes. A copy with o<32, l<4: 9 bits for up
 to 4 bytes (vs 4×12.4 ≈ 50 bits) — a big saving. To reach ≤ 2500 bytes: I need raw
 bits ≤ about 20000 (allowing for the adaptive overhead, about 2500×8 = 20000).

  A rough budget: let x = the number of copies (average 9-13 bits), y = the number of literals (
average ~12.4): x + y = j, x·(average copy length L) + y = 4868. If the average L = 
3 (many short copies): x·3 + y = 4868, x + y = j. Bits: x·11 + y·12.4 ≈ j·12 + x·...
 let me parameterize: y = 4868 - 3x (if L=3), j = x + y = 4868 - 2x. Bits ≈ x·11 + (
4868 - 3x)·12.4 = 11x + 60363 - 37.2x = 60363 - 26.2x. Need ≤ 20000 → x ≥ 1550 copies, each
 ≥ 3 bytes... x = 1550: y = 4868 - 4650 = 218. j = 1768. Hmm, 1550 matches of length ≥ 
3 in 4868 bytes of text: is that possible? It depends on the data. If the text is repetitive
 (like repeated paragraphs), yes.

  But copies with l < 4 are only "cheap" in the sense that k_l = 0 (l ≤ 3). Longer copies
: l ∈ [4, 7] → k_l = 1: 5 bits; l ∈ [8,15] → k_l=2: 7 bits... a long copy with a large off:
 the off is large → k_o grows.

  Alternatively, mixed: a few long copies + many literals. The optimization must be
 data-driven. Let me look at the data first — I'll read data.txt (4868 bytes, fine to
 read in full — it's small).

  And implement:
  1. A program builder (a greedy/DP matcher).
  2. The exact arithmetic encoder (as above) + the resync count.
  3. A verifier against the real binary.
  4. An optimizer: start with a reasonable program, then local improvements: try alternative choices
 at each position (a copy with a different off/len, or a literal), keep it if the
 measured resync count decreases. This is expensive: the resync count is computed per full
 program (a Python simulation of ~30k bits — fast enough: ~50ms per evaluation). The local
 search over 5000 positions × a few alternatives × a few passes ≈ manageable.

  A better way: a DP that computes the exact cost with a surrogate cost that correlates well
 with the resync count... risky. Let me go with greedy + local search with the real cost function
.

  Actually, let me think more carefully about the cost function. The resync count is determined by
 the range trajectory: the product of the per-bit p factors. A resync happens when
 the range < 255. p = (c0+1)/(c0+c1+2): adaptive. Hard to decompose. I'll just measure it.


  Plan:
  Step 0: read data.txt in full; check for NULs; check the statistics.
  Step 1: write the encoder (a Python module): program → byte sequence; also a function
 to compute the resync count; verify the invariant (the backward interval) — if at
 some resync the valid d set is empty → an error (shouldn't happen).
  Step 2: a quick test: encode a small known program, and decode with the real binary, to
 verify the framework. (e.g., a j-symbol program that produces a short string; check
 the round-trip.)
  Step 3: build the initial program for data.txt: a greedy: at each position Q: find the best
 (off, len) copy among off ∈ [1, Q] (with an off ≤ 256 limit for speed), len ≥ 1, matching S
[Q:Q+len] == S[Q-off:Q-off+len] (the overlap-safe comparison: compare character by character with
 the overlap), prefer... then compare the cost: a copy vs a literal. Since the cost is context
-dependent (the arithmetic state), a purely local greedy may be suboptimal, but it
's a good start. Actually, for the initial program: for each position: if there's
 a match of length ≥ 2 with off ≤ 255, use the longest such copy (the standard greedy), otherwise a
 literal.
  Step 4: measure the size. If > 2500: local search: for each symbol, try: literal ↔ copy (a few off/
len options), keep the improvement. Repeat the passes until no improvement or the time budget
.
  Step 5: write /app/data.comp; verify: `cat /app/data.comp | /app/decomp | cmp - /app
/data.txt`; confirm the size ≤ 2500.

  ```c
  int get_integer(int tmp, int ctx) {
    int subtract_it = 1<<tmp;
    int result_ans = 1;
    ctx*=99;
    while (!get_bit(++tmp+ctx));
    tmp--;
    W {
      result_ans = result_ans*2 | get_bit(ctx);
    }
    return result_ans - subtract_it;
  }
  ```

  But note: the arithmetic coder's efficiency depends on the context model's adaptation; the first use of each context has a +1 prior (a split with c0=c1=0: split = range * 1/2). The contexts are used sparsely (the ctx 0 W-bits are used for the j-mantissa; ctx 1 is per symbol; ctx 8 is per literal; ctx 198/203+ for copies; ctx 297/300+ for len; ctx 891/896+ for literals' mag). The model is a simple per-context bit bias, adapting with counts — it's like a "bit-level adaptive" coder: it can at best exploit correlations that the per-context counts capture (marginal bit biases). So the achievable size ≈ the sum over contexts of (number of bits at that context) * (redundancy based on the marginal bias). The coder cannot exploit cross-bit structure beyond what the contexts capture.

  So the program choice should match what the model can capture: e.g., a literal mag for text: B-bits at ctx 891: their distribution depends on the character set. The coder will learn the marginal bias of each B-bit... but ctx 891 is the same context for all mag B-bits! So all B bits of mags (across all literals, all positions) share one context. The bias learned will be the average. Similarly, off B-bits share ctx 198; len B-bits share ctx 297; j B-bits share ctx 0; the while-loop bits: each context is its own (203+k, 896+k, etc.) — sparse.

  Now, let me plan the encoder:

  ```python
  # Simulate the decoder's contexts exactly
  class Coder:
      def __init__(self):
          self.counts = {}  # ctx -> [c0, c1]
          self.range = 1
          self.frac = 0     # the decoder's fraction — I track this to emit digits
          self.lo = 0
          self.hi = 1       # valid interval on the current scale (255^D)
          self.out = bytearray()

      def ctx(self, c):
          if c not in self.counts: self.counts[c] = [0, 0]
          return self.counts[c]

      def emit_digit(self):
          # Need to choose d such that [lo*255, hi*255) ⊆ [255*frac + d, 255*frac + d + 255*range)
          lo_bound = 255*(hi - self.frac - self.range)  # d >= this
          hi_bound = 255*(self.lo - self.frac)          # d <= this
          # clamp to 0..254
          d = max(0, lo_bound)
          if d > hi_bound or d > 254:
              # fallback: should not happen; try another
              ...
          self.out.append(d + 1)
          self.frac = 255*self.frac + d
          self.range = 255*self.range
          # shift the interval: it's on the new scale
          self.lo *= 255
          self.hi *= 255

      def get_bit_enc(self, ctx, bit):
          if self.range < 255:
              self.emit_digit()
          c = self.ctx(ctx)
          c0, c1 = c
          split = self.range * (c0 + 1) // (c0 + c1 + 2)
          # match the decoder's integer arithmetic exactly: the decoder does range * -~*counts / (sum + 2)
          # all int; Python's // matches C's int division for non-negative values
          if bit == 0:
              self.hi = self.lo + split
          else:
              self.lo = self.lo + split
          c[bit] += 1
          # note: the decoder's range/fract update is mirrored only via the invariant; but I also need
          # to update frac/range to mirror the decoder! Wait...
  ```

  So in get_bit_enc:
  ```
  if self.range < 255: emit_digit()   # the decoder does exactly this at the start of get_bit
  ```

  The standard solution (and the reason for the "interval" approach): the set of valid d's is an interval; if I choose d wisely, the set of "valid frac prefixes" stays consistent. The clean, provably correct approach: maintain the interval [lo, hi) of frac values (on the current scale) that are "still viable" — i.e., there exists a continuation of digits (including the current one, to be chosen) that satisfies all future constraints... that's getting into the weeds.

  Now, the program-search problem. Let me define:
  - S = data.txt, N = 4868.
  - A program: a sequence of j symbols; each symbol: L(b) literal byte b, or C(off, len) (off ≤ the current position Q, len ≥ 1, S[Q:Q+len] = S[Q-off:Q-off+len] (with the overlap semantics: copy one byte at a time: out[Q+i] = out[Q-off+i]).
  - Constraint: the total output = exactly N. (The j symbols are free.)
  - Cost (approximate): the number of resyncs in the arithmetic stream of the bit program.

  Bit program for a symbol:
  - Literal L(b): bit0: ctx 1, bit 0. bit1: sign: ctx 8, bit s (s = 1 if I choose the negative sign... (1-2*bit): bit=0 → +1, bit=1 → -1). mag = get_integer(4, 9): m = mag: k = 0 if m < 16, otherwise the smallest k such that m < 2^(4+k)... m ∈ [2^(4+k-1), 2^(4+k)) → k. Bits: k zeros (ctx 896+k... wait the while contexts: tmp0=4, ctx_param=9 → base ctx = 4*... `ctx*=99`: ctx = 891. while: ++tmp + ctx: first tmp = 5 → 896. So the contexts: 896, 897, ..., 895+k. Iteration k+1 (the one that reads the 1): tmp = 4 + (k+1) → ctx = 895 + k + ... 4 + k + 1 + 891 = 896 + k. So the zeros are at ctx 896..895+k... the k zeros: ctx 896, 897, ..., 895+k; the 1 at ctx 896 + k. Then the W-loop: tmp after the loop = 4 + k + 1; tmp-- → 4 + k; the W-bits: (4+k) bits at ctx 891: B = m - (2^k - 1)·16, width 4+k.
    - Note: m = 0 → k = 0: the 1-bit at ctx 896, 4 B-bits at 891 (all 0).
    - The sign choice: for b ∈ [0,127]: m = b, sign + (bit 0 at ctx 8). For b ∈ [128, 255]: m = 256 - b, sign - (bit 1 at ctx 8). (The alternative for b < 128: sign -, m = 256 + b → larger. I choose the minimum m.)
  - Copy C(off, len): bit at ctx 1: 1. off: get_integer(5, 2): o = off - 1: k(o): 0 if o < 32, otherwise the smallest k such that o < 2^(5+k): o ∈ [2^(5+k-1), 2^(5+k)).   The raw bit count of a symbol: literal: 1 + 1 + (1 + k_m + 4 + k_m) = 7 + 2·k_m... wait: the while: k zeros + 1 = k+1 bits; the W: 4 + k. Total mag bits = k + 1 + 4 + k = 5 + 2k.   This is a substantial combinatorial optimization. Let me simplify:

  Insight: the cost in resyncs ≈ the total entropy under the adaptive per-context model. A good program: use copies whenever there's a match (even short ones with a small off), and use literals otherwise. The j symbols: a mix.

  Also, I should consider that j (the number of symbols) is coded at the start: j = the total number of symbols, known only after the program is built. No problem.

  Let me think about the scale of the resync count: each resync occurs when the range drops below 255. The range evolution: at each bit, range is multiplied by p or (1-p), where p = (c0+1)/(c0+c1+2) (the probability of 0). The resyncs are like "normalization events": roughly, the number of resyncs ≈ the number of bits / log2(255) ≈ bits/8 · ... each resync consumes log2(255) ≈ 7.996 bits of "range precision". So bytes ≈ total_bits / 8 (asymptotically, ignoring the adaptive overhead). So size ≈ raw bit count / 8 + a little. Raw bit count for all-literals: 4868 symbols × (7 + 2k_m) + the j code (j = 4868: k_J: 4868 ∈ [2^12=4096, 2^13=8192) → k=3 (2^(9+k) > 4868: 2^12 → k=3: [2^12, 2^13): J ∈ [2^(9+3-1)=4096, 8192): yes k=3) → j code: 1 + 3 + 12 = 16 bits. Total ≈ 4868·(average 7 + 2·k_m) + 16. The average k_m for text: the byte distribution: for printable text, m: most bytes in [32,126] → k=2 (32-63) or 3 (64-127): roughly half each, plus some k=0 (space? space = 32 → m=32 → k=2; hmm m=0..15: which bytes give m < 16? b ∈ [0,15] or b ∈ [241,255] — rare in text). So the average k_m ≈ 2.7 → average literal bits ≈ 12.4 → total ≈ 4868·12.4 + 16 ≈ 60,400 bits ≈ 7550 bytes. Way over 2500.

  With copies: each copy replaces len literal bytes. A copy with o<32, l<4: 9 bits for up to 4 bytes (vs 4×12.4 ≈ 50 bits) — a big saving. To reach ≤ 2500 bytes: I need raw bits ≤ about 20000 (allowing for the adaptive overhead, about 2500×8 = 20000).

  A rough budget: let x = the number of copies (average 9-13 bits), y = the number of literals (average ~12.4): x + y = j, x·(average copy length L) + y = 4868. get_integer(tmp, ctx):
```

ctx*=99;

tmp--;

```

Range coder:

```
int get_bit(int ctx) {
  if (range < radix) { range *= radix; fraction *= radix; fraction += gc()-1; }
  int *counts = cc + ctx*2;
  int split = range * -~*counts / (*counts + counts[1] + 2);
  int the_bit = fraction >= split;
  fraction -= split*the_bit;
  range = the_bit ? range-split : split;
  counts[the_bit]++;
  return the_bit;
}
```

Note: `fraction` and `range` are global `int`. `range` is `long range = 1`. fraction is int (

5.

split = range * -~counts0 / (counts0 + counts1 + 2). -~counts0 = counts0 + 1. So split = range*(c0
+1)/(c0+c1+2). Standard 3-value normalization (total + 2, with 1 reserved for each side —
 actually split = range*(c0+1)/(c0+c1+2) is the standard "interval" formula where bit=0
 gets [0, split) and bit=1 gets [split, range)).

the_bit = fraction >= split. So bit 0 when fraction < split, bit 1 when fraction >= split
.

fraction -= split * the_bit. range = the_bit ? range - split : split.

```python
# sim.py: mirror the C decoder
class Sim:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.pos = 0
        self.F = 0  # fraction
        self.R = 1  # range
        self.cc = [0, 0] * 1000000
    def gc(self):
        c = self.data[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.data) else 0xFF
        self.pos += 1
        return c
    def get_bit(self, ctx):
        if self.R < 255:
            self.R *= 255
            self.F = self.F * 255 + self.gc() - 1
        n0, n1 = self.cc[ctx*2], self.cc[ctx*2+1]
        S = self.R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        b = 1 if self.F >= S else 0
        if b:
            self.F -= S
            self.R -= S
        else:
            self.R = S
        self.cc[ctx*2 + b] += 1
        return b
    def get_integer(self, L, ctx):
        # mirror exactly
        subtract_it = 1 << L
        result_ans = 1
        ctx2 = ctx * 99
        tmp = L
        while True:
            tmp += 1
            if self.get_bit(tmp + ctx2):
                break
        tmp -= 1
        for _ in range(tmp):
            result_ans = result_ans * 2 | self.get_bit(ctx2)
        return result_ans - subtract_it
    def decode(self):
        j = self.get_integer(9, 0)
        out = bytearray()
        for _ in range(j):
            if self.get_bit(1):
                o = self.get_integer(5, 2)
                z = len(out) - o - 1
                L = self.get_integer(2, 3) + 1
                out += out[z:z+L]   # note: overlap is fine
            else:
                sgn = self.get_bit(8)
                v = self.get_integer(4, 9)
                out.append(((1 - 2*sgn) * v) & 0xFF)
        return bytes(out), j
```

Wait — C: `z = Q - get_integer(OFF1, 2) - 1; tmp = get_integer(OFF2, 3) + 1;` — note: if o is too large, z could point before the start of the buffer — undefined behavior in C; my partitioner guarantees o + 1 ≤ the current length ✓. Also C: `*Q++ = *z++` copy with overlap: a forward copy: out[z:z+L] in Python: if z + L > the current end: the C code reads garbage (an uninitialized buf) — I must guarantee z + L ≤ the current end ✓ (the match is within the already-output prefix).

Hmm, one thing: in C, `int split = range * -~*counts / (*counts + counts[1] + 2);`: operator precedence: `range * -~*counts`: unary: -~(*counts) = -(~counts) = counts + 1 ✓. Then `range * (counts+1) / (tot)`: ((range * (counts+1)) / tot): range is long: the product is long: no overflow ✓. Division: /: integer, truncation toward zero; all positive → floor ✓.

Ah — I need the ctx of each bit. The bit stream must be structured: I'll generate the "bit plan": a list of (bit, ctx) in order. Then the skeleton simulation:

```python
def skeleton(bitctx):
    R = 1; depth = 0
    cc = [[0,0] for _ in range(1000000)]  # a dict would be better
    events = []
    for (b, ctx) in bitctx:
        if R < 255:
            R *= 255; depth += 1
        n0, n1 = cc[ctx]
        S = R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        events.append((depth, b, S, R))
        if b:
            R -= S
            cc[ctx][1] += 1
        else:
            R = S
            cc[ctx][0] += 1
    return events, depth
```

Wait, note: the C code: the scale happens inside get_bit before split ✓. The counts are incremented after the bit ✓.

Hmm, one issue: the R after the final event is unused. ✓.

Then the cuts: for each event (depth d, bit b, S, R):
- A = (S * 255**d + R - 1) // R (i.e., ceil(S·255^d / R))
- b=0: high = min(high, A / 255^d)
- b=1: low = max(low, A / 255^d)

Using Fraction: A and 255^d are integers: the cut value = Fraction(A, 255**d). 255**d for d up to ~5000: huge (5000·log10(255) ≈ 14400 digits). The Fraction ops: OK but somewhat slow. Optimization: I'll keep low and high as Fractions; each event does 1-2 comparisons and min/max — cheap enough. ~40k events → no problem.

```python
import sys
data = open('/app/data.txt','rb').read()
n = len(data)

def k_for(L, v):  # the smallest k with v <= (2**k - 1) << L - 1
    # (2^k - 1)*2^L - 1 >= v
    k = 1
    while (2**k - 1) * (1 << L) - 1 < v:
        k += 1
    return k

def int_bits(L, v):
    return 2 * k_for(L, v) + L - 1
```

```python
import sys, math
from fractions import Fraction

DATA = open('/app/data.txt','rb').read()
N = len(DATA)

# ---------- the exact C-mirroring simulation (for verification) ----------
class Sim:
    __slots__ = ('data','pos','F','R','cc')
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data; self.pos = 0; self.F = 0; self.R = 1
        self.cc = [[0,0] for _ in range(2048)]  # the max ctx used is 891; 2048*2 is enough
    def gc(self):
        c = self.data[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.data) else 0xFF
        self.pos += 1
        return c
    def get_bit(self, ctx):
        if self.R < 255:
            self.R *= 255
            self.F = self.F * 255 + self.gc() - 1
        c0 = self.cc[ctx][0]; c1 = self.cc[ctx][1]
        S = self.R * (c0 + 1) // (c0 + c1 + 2)
        b = 1 if self.F >= S else 0
        if b:
            self.F -= S; self.R -= S; self.cc[ctx][1] = c1 + 1
        else:
            self.R = S; self.cc[ctx][0] = c0 + 1
        return b
    def get_integer(self, L, ctx):
        subtract_it = 1 << L
        result_ans = 1
        ctx2 = ctx * 99
        tmp = L
        while True:
            tmp += 1
            if self.get_bit(tmp + ctx2):
                break
        tmp -= 1
        for _ in range(tmp):
            result_ans = result_ans * 2 | self.get_bit(ctx2)
        return result_ans - subtract_it
    def decode(self):
        j = self.get_integer(9, 0)
        out = bytearray()
        Q = 0
        for _ in range(j):
            if self.get_bit(1):
                o = self.get_integer(5, 2)
                L = self.get_integer(2, 3) + 1
                z = Q - o - 1
                if z < 0 or z + L > Q:
                    raise RuntimeError('bad copy')
                out += out[z:z+L]
                Q += L
            else:
                sgn = self.get_bit(8)
                v = self.get_integer(4, 9)
                val = (1 - 2*sgn) * v
                out.append(val & 0xFF)
                Q += 1
        if Q != N:  # for the random test, N is passed separately
            pass
        return bytes(out)
```

The bit plan and the skeleton:

```python
def k_for(L, v):
    # the smallest k >= 1 with v <= (2**k - 1)*(1<<L) - 1
    k = 1
    while (2**k - 1) * (1 << L) - 1 < v:
        k += 1
    return k

def integer_bits(L, v, ctx):
    # returns a list of (bit, ctx) for get_integer(L, ctx)
    k = k_for(L, v)
    ans = v + (1 << L)
    out = []
    # the leading: k-1 zeros at ctxs L+1 .. L+k-1, then a 1 at ctx L+k
    for c in range(L + 1, L + k):
        out.append((0, c))
    out.append((1, L + k))
    # the trailing: the low (L + k - 1) bits of ans, MSB first, all at ctx*99
    ntr = L + k - 1
    c99 = ctx * 99
    for b in range(ntr - 1, -1, -1):
        out.append(((ans >> b) & 1, c99))
    return out
```

Verify: ans = v + 2^L ∈ [2^L, 2^(L+k) - 1]: bit length = L + k: bits L+k-1 .. 0: the top bit (position L+k-1) = 1: the trailing is ntr = L + k - 1 bits = positions L+k-2 down to 0 ✓. Total bits: (k-1) + 1 + (L+k-1) = 2k + L - 1 ✓ matches int_bits.

Verify the k boundaries: v = (2^k - 1)·2^L - 1: ans = v + 2^L = 2^L(2^k - 1) + 2^L - ... = 2^(L+k) - 2^L + 2^L - 1 = 2^(L+k) - 1: the bit length L+k ✓ fits. v = 0: ans = 2^L: the trailing: the low L bits are 0 ✓.

```python
def block_bitplan(blocks, N):
    # blocks: a list of ('C', o, L) or ('L', v)
    bits = []
    j = len(blocks)
    bits += integer_bits(9, j, 0)
    for b in blocks:
        if b[0] == 'C':
            bits.append((1, 1))
            bits += integer_bits(5, b[1], 2)
            bits += integer_bits(2, b[2] - 1, 3)
        else:
            bits.append((0, 1))
            bits.append((0, 8))   # positive
            bits += integer_bits(4, b[1], 9)
    return bits
```

The sign: for a positive v: sgn bit = 0: (1 - 2·0)·v = +v ✓.

The skeleton + the encoder:

```python
def skeleton(bits):
    R = 1; depth = 0
    cc = {}
    events = []
    for (b, ctx) in bits:
        if R < 255:
            R *= 255; depth += 1
        n0, n1 = cc.get(ctx, (0, 0))
        S = R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        events.append((depth, b, S, R))
        if b:
            R -= S
            cc[ctx] = (n0, n1 + 1)
        else:
            R = S
            cc[ctx] = (n0 + 1, n1)
    return events, depth

def encode(bits):
    events, depth = skeleton(bits)
    low = Fraction(0, 1); high = Fraction(1, 1)
    for (d, b, S, R) in events:
        A = (S * 255**d + R - 1) // R   # ceil(S*255^d / R)
        val = Fraction(A, 255**d)
        if b == 0:
            if val < high: high = val
        else:
            if val > low: low = val
        if low >= high:
            return None
    # pick x*
    x = (low + high) / 2
    # uniqueness check: is x of the form m/255^d?
    def safe(x):
        p, q = x.numerator, x.denominator
        while q % 3 == 0: q //= 3
        while q % 5 == 0: q //= 5
        return q != 1
    candidates = [x, (low + 2*high)/3, (2*low + high)/3, (low + high)/4 ...]
```

Hmm, more simply: if not safe(x): try a few. Actually, if low = A/255^d1, high = B/255^d2: the midpoint's denominator = 2·255^k (after reduction) → always has a factor of 2 → always safe. Prove: low = p/q1, q1 = 255^a (in lowest terms: A/255^d: after reduction the denominator divides 255^d: yes, the denominator is 255^a (a ≤ d)). Similarly high = B/255^c. Midpoint = (p·255^c + B·255^a) / (2·255^{a+c}): reduction: the denominator is 2·255^k (k ≤ a + c)... the numerator may share factors of 3, 5 with 255^{a+c}, but the factor of 2: the numerator p·255^c + B·255^a = 255^a·(p·255^{c-a} + B), if c ≥ a: the numerator is divisible by 255^a... the factor of 2: 255^a·(odd) + ...: the numerator is odd·...: p is odd? p could be even: 255^a is odd: p·255^c: the parity = the parity of p. The numerator = p·255^c + B·255^a: the parity = p + B (mod 2) (odd factors don't matter). If p + B is even: the numerator is even → the factor of 2
 cancels → the denominator = 255^k: not safe! In that case the midpoint = an integer/255^k: a finite expansion: ambiguous. Now, the partitioner:

```python
def lcp(a, b, i, limit):
    # the LCP of a[i:] and a[b:]
    k = 0
    while i + k < limit and b + k < limit and a[i+k] == a[b+k]:
        k += 1
    return k
```

```python
        tot = n0 + n1 + 2
        p = (n_b + 1) / tot ...
```

The cost function: I'll simulate the block's bits through the model (in the correct order), accumulating -log2 p:

```python
import math
def block_cost(block, i, model):
    if block[0] == 'L':
        bits = [(0,1), (0,8)] + integer_bits(4, block[1], 9)
    else:
        bits = [(1,1)] + integer_bits(5, block[1], 2) + integer_bits(2, block[2]-1, 3)
    cost = 0.0
    for (b, ctx) in bits:
        n0, n1 = model.get(ctx, (0,0))
        tot = n0 + n1 + 2
        p = (n0 + 1) / tot if b == 0 else (n1 + 1) / tot
        cost += -math.log2(p)
        if b == 0: model[ctx] = (n0 + 1, n1)
        else: model[ctx] = (n0, n1 + 1)
    return cost
```

Note: the model update must reflect the actual bits ✓.

- Option B: local search on the blocks.

Actually, one more consideration on the `int split = range * -~*counts / (*counts + counts[1] + 2);` in C — I'll re-verify the `-~*counts` parsing: the unary operator `-` applies to `~*counts`: `~
*counts` = ~counts[0]: the int: ~x = -x - 1: -(~counts[0]) = counts[0] + 1 ✓.

Now let's understand the format:

- The stream is a range coder: 8-bit symbols (values 1..255, i.e., gc() - 1).
- There are models `cc[ctx*2]`, counts[0], counts[1] for bit-models at various contexts.
- First: `j = get_integer(9, 0)` — the number of blocks.
- Then, for each of the j blocks:
  - `get_bit(1)` — flag. If 1: copy. `offset = get_integer(OFF1=5, ctx=2)`, `z = Q - offset - 1`, `tmp = get_integer(OFF2=2, ctx=3) + 1` = length. Copy length bytes from position z.
  - If 0: literal. `bit = get_bit(8)`; `val = get_integer(LITSIZE=4, ctx=9)`; `*Q++ = (1-2*bit)*val`.

get_integer(tmp, ctx):
```
int subtract_it = 1<<tmp;
int result_ans = 1;
ctx*=99;
while (!get_bit(++tmp+ctx));   // read bits until 1 is read (tmp gets incremented on each read)
tmp--;
for (i=0;i<tmp;i++) result_ans = result_ans*2 | get_bit(ctx);
return result_ans - subtract_it;
```

Let's parse carefully. The initial value of the parameter `tmp` is, say, L. `subtract_it = 1<<L`. `result_ans=1`. `ctx *= 99` (ctx is the argument's ctx). Then loop: `while (!get_bit(++tmp+ctx))`. So on each iteration, tmp is incremented first (so the first call is at context L+1, then L+2, ...), and get_bit is called until it returns 1. Note that after the loop ends, tmp has been incremented past the position of the 1. Then `tmp--` (so tmp = position of the 1, in the sense that the 1-th call happened at original L + k times... let's define: after the while loop, tmp = L + (number of get_bit calls). Since the loop increments tmp at the start of each iteration, and the loop condition is evaluated k times (k bits read, last of which is 1). At the k-th evaluation, tmp has been incremented k times, so tmp = L + k. Then tmp-- gives L + k - 1 = the number of bits read - 1... no, k is the total number of get_bit calls. tmp-- → L+k-1. Then the loop `for (i=0;i<tmp;i++)` executes L+k-1 times? That seems wrong.

Hmm, wait, that would read more bits than expected. Let me re-verify: standard "variable-length coding": read bits MSB-first until 1 (the leading 1), then read the remaining (n-1) bits. Total = value in [1, 2^(n+1)-1] with a bias.

Standard form: `result=1; while(!get_bit()) { result=2*result+1... }` hmm.

- subtract_it = 1 << L.
- result_ans = 1.
- while (!get_bit(++tmp+ctx)); → read bits until 1 is read. Each bit read at this stage... but note result_ans is not updated in this loop. Interesting.
- tmp-- → now tmp = L + k - 1, where k = number of bits read in the first phase.
- W { result_ans = result_ans*2 | get_bit(ctx); } → executed tmp times = L + k - 1 times, all at context ctx (constant).
- return result_ans - subtract_it.

So total bits: k + L + k - 1 = 2k + L - 1. Value range: result_ans is in [2^(L+k-1), 2^(L+k) - 1]. Subtracting 1<<L.

Hmm, so this is a strange coding. Let's think about the range of possible return values. Let k ≥ 1 (at least the terminating 1). result_ans ∈ [2^(L+k-1), 2^(L+k)-1]. return = result_ans - 2^L ∈ [2^(L+k-1) - 2^L, 2^(L+k) - 1 - 2^L].

For k=1: [2^(L-1) - 2^L, 2^L - 1 - 2^L] = [-2^(L-1), -1].
For k=2: [2^L - 2^L, 2^(L+1)-1-2^L] = [0, 2^L - 1].

So the value v satisfies: k = 1 + floor(log2(...))? The ranges partition: [-2^(L-1), -1], [0, 2^L-1], [2^L, 3·2^L-1], [3·2^L, 7·2^L-1], ...

Interesting. So to encode a value v (given L): find k such that v falls in [ (2^(k-1) - 1)·2^L... let's compute the lower bound of range k: LB_k = 2^(L+k-1) - 2^L = 2^L(2^(k-1) - 1). Upper bound UB_k = 2^(L+k) - 1 - 2^L = 2^L(2^k - 1) - 1.

k=1: LB = 2^L(1-1)=0? Wait 2^(L+0) - 2^L = 0. Hmm: 2^(L+k-1) with k=1 is 2^L. 2^L - 2^L = 0. So range for k=1: [0 - ... wait, I made a computation error above.

k=2: LB_2 = 2^(L+2-1) - 2^L = 2^(L+1) - 2^L = 2^L. UB_2 = 2^(L+2) - 1 - 2^L = 3·2^L - 1. Range [2^L, 3·2^L-1].

Great! So any value v ≥ 0: k = 1 + floor(log2(1 + v/2^L)). That is, if 0 ≤ v < 2^L then k=1; else v ≥ (2^(k-1)-1)2^L.

And the encoding: first phase: k-1 zero bits followed by 1 bit. Wait: in get_integer, the first loop reads bits until 1. If we want total k bits in the first phase, we write (k-1) zeros followed by 1. But the contexts differ: the i-th bit (i from 1..k) is at context L + i. The remaining L + k - 1 bits are all at context ctx (= original ctx × 99), and they form result_ans = (1 followed by L+k-1 bits) = a number in [2^(L+k-1), 2^(L+k)-1], and v = that - 2^L.

So given v and L:
- Compute k = smallest integer such that v ≤ (2^k - 1)·2^L - 1.
- First phase bits: 0×(k-1), then 1. (Contexts L+1 .. L+k, bit i at context L+i.)
- Remaining: result_ans = v + 2^L, in binary is 1 followed by L+k-1 bits; write the remaining L+k-1 bits at context ctx99 = ctx*99.

Now contexts: what are the ctx values?

- get_integer(9, 0): tmp=9, ctx=0 → ctx99=0. Leading-bit contexts: 10, 11, ..., 9+k. Remaining bits at context 0.
- Copy: get_bit(1) flag.

- Literal: get_bit(8) sign; get_integer(LITSIZE=4, 9): leading contexts 5..4+k; remaining at ctx 9*99=891.

All context indices: 0..9 are used directly (0,1,2,3,5,8,9) and 198, 297, 891. cc is int cc[1000000] → 4MB, no problem.

Range coder:

```
int get_bit(int ctx) {
  if (range < radix) { range *= radix; fraction *= radix; fraction += gc()-1; }
  int *counts = cc + ctx*2;
  int split = range * -~*counts / (*counts + counts[1] + 2);
  int the_bit = fraction >= split;
  fraction -= split*the_bit;
  range = the_bit ? range-split : split;
  counts[the_bit]++;
  return the_bit;
}
```

Note: `fraction` and `range` are global `int`. `range` is `long range = 1`. fraction is int (global, initialized to 0). At start, range=1 < 255 → range*=255=255, fraction*=255=0, fraction += gc()-1. So the first symbol c ∈ [0,255] is read as gc()-1 ∈ [0,254]? gc() returns an unsigned char in [0,255], so gc()-1 ∈ [-1, 254]. Hmm, c is an unsigned char: 0..255, c-1: -1..254. Interesting: so fraction starts at c-1 ∈ [-1, 254], and range=255.

split = range * -~counts0 / (counts0 + counts1 + 2). -~counts0 = counts0 + 1. So split = range*(c0+1)/(c0+c1+2). Standard 3-value normalization (total + 2, with 1 reserved for each side — actually split = range*(c0+1)/(c0+c1+2) is the standard "interval" formula where bit=0 gets [0, split) and bit=1 gets [split, range)).

the_bit = fraction >= split. So bit 0 when fraction < split, bit 1 when fraction >= split.

fraction -= split * the_bit. range = the_bit ? range - split : split.

Standard. To encode: maintain (range, fraction). Initially range=1, fraction=0. On decoding: if range < 255: range *= 255, fraction = fraction*255 + (c-1) where c is the raw byte (0..255), c-1 ∈ [-1..254].

To encode: for each bit, we have the interval [lower, lower+range) within [0, 255). Hmm, the standard formulation: x ∈ [0,1) with fraction/range. Initially fraction=0, range=1 → x=0. The encoder mirrors this: at each get_bit:

4. The hard part: the block partitioning. I need a partition of data.txt into ~B blocks: each block = a copy (offset, length) or a literal byte. j = number of blocks. I need total compressed size ≤ 2500 bytes.

I'll implement an LZ77-style search: maintain a dictionary of previous positions (a suffix search via hashing): for each position i, find the best (offset, length) copy: offset ∈ [1, i], i.e., the data has an offset up to the current output length; find the longest match with the best... the cost model: I need an accurate cost model: the bits per block ≈ flag + offset_bits(offset) + length_bits(len) + (the model-adaptive entropy of each bit — I'll approximate: I'll count each bit at ~its entropy under a simple stationary approximation... more simply: I'll approximate the total bits as: sum over all bits of [-log2 p(context)], p from a quick simulation of the model counts. That's essentially a 2-pass approach: simulate the bit stream with the model, count bits, weight by the model's -log probability. That gives the expected byte count ≈ bits/8 + a small fudge. Then I'll iterate: adjust the partition to reduce the bit count.

I'll just simulate the model live: for candidate blocks, I can compute the exact bit count (the k values) and approximate the model's cost via a running count estimate. Since the range coder with adaptive models ≈ arithmetic on the per-context binary entropy, the total bytes ≈ Σ_ctx [binary entropy of the bit sequence in that context] / 8 + a small overhead (~few bits/context... plus the integer-coding overhead is included in the bits).

Plan: I'll compute the exact bit stream for a partition, and estimate the byte count by a "model-aware" count: I'll simulate the model counts while decoding my bit stream, and at each bit, accumulate -log2(p_model), where p_model = (n_b + 1/2)/(n_0+n_1+2) (Laplace — matches the range coder's interval widths: split/range → (n0+1)/(n0+n1+2)). The range coder's output ≈ that sum (within a small factor). Then bytes ≈ ceil(that sum / 8) + 1-2. I'll aim for ~1950-2050 estimated bits·... aim for ≤ ~2000 bytes with margin.

Alternatively, I can do a better thing: I can do an exact encoding (my interval method gives the exact bytes for a fixed bit stream). So the pipeline: partition → bit stream → exact byte count via the encoder (the encoder gives the exact number of bytes emitted! the interval method emits exactly the bytes needed — the count is exact, not an estimate). So: I'll try a partition, run the exact encoder, and check len(bytes) ≤ 2500. If it's over, I'll improve the partition. The exact encoder is the truth — no estimation needed.

So the algorithm:
- Phase 1: find a partition with a reasonable greedy LZ (a cost model using exact k computation + approximate model weights), e.g., maximize the match length / minimize the cost.
- Phase 2: encode exactly; if it's ≤ 2500, done; otherwise refine the partition (e.g., local optimization: try alternative offsets/lengths, or different literal groupings).

LZ details: the data is 4868 bytes of English text: high redundancy: typical LZ77 (window = the entire file, max length 10000) gives ~2.5-3 bytes per block... the constraint: a block's offset ≤ current position (z = Q - offset - 1 ≥ start of the buffer: offset + 1 ≤ Q - start: offset ≤ i, i = the number of bytes output so far). The cost model per block type:

```python
# sim.py: mirror the C decoder
class Sim:
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data
        self.pos = 0
        self.F = 0  # fraction
        self.R = 1  # range
        self.cc = [0, 0] * 1000000
    def gc(self):
        c = self.data[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.data) else 0xFF
        self.pos += 1
        return c
    def get_bit(self, ctx):
        if self.R < 255:
            self.R *= 255
            self.F = self.F * 255 + self.gc() - 1
        n0, n1 = self.cc[ctx*2], self.cc[ctx*2+1]
        S = self.R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        b = 1 if self.F >= S else 0
        if b:
            self.F -= S
            self.R -= S
        else:
            self.R = S
        self.cc[ctx*2 + b] += 1
        return b
    def get_integer(self, L, ctx):
        # mirror exactly
        subtract_it = 1 << L
        result_ans = 1
        ctx2 = ctx * 99
        tmp = L
        while True:
            tmp += 1
            if self.get_bit(tmp + ctx2):
                break
        tmp -= 1
        for _ in range(tmp):
            result_ans = result_ans * 2 | self.get_bit(ctx2)
        return result_ans - subtract_it
    def decode(self):
        j = self.get_integer(9, 0)
        out = bytearray()
        for _ in range(j):
            if self.get_bit(1):
                o = self.get_integer(5, 2)
                z = len(out) - o - 1
                L = self.get_integer(2, 3) + 1
                out += out[z:z+L]   # note: overlap is fine
            else:
                sgn = self.get_bit(8)
                v = self.get_integer(4, 9)
                out.append(((1 - 2*sgn) * v) & 0xFF)
        return bytes(out), j
```

Wait — C: `z = Q - get_integer(OFF1, 2) - 1; tmp = get_integer(OFF2, 3) + 1;` — note: if o is too large, z could point before the start of the buffer — undefined behavior in C; my partitioner guarantees o + 1 ≤ the current length ✓. Also C: `*Q++ = *z++` copy with overlap: a forward copy: out[z:z+L] in Python: if z + L > the current end: the C code reads garbage (an uninitialized buf) — I must guarantee z + L ≤ the current end ✓ (the match is within the already-output prefix).

Hmm, one thing: in C, `int split = range * -~*counts / (*counts + counts[1] + 2);`: operator precedence: `range * -~*counts`: unary: -~(*counts) = -(~counts) = counts + 1 ✓. Then `range * (counts+1) / (tot)`: ((range * (counts+1)) / tot): range is long: the product is long: no overflow ✓. Division: /: integer, truncation toward zero; all positive → floor ✓.

Ah — I need the ctx of each bit. The bit stream must be structured: I'll generate the "bit plan": a list of (bit, ctx) in order. Then the skeleton simulation:

```python
def skeleton(bitctx):
    R = 1; depth = 0
    cc = [[0,0] for _ in range(1000000)]  # a dict would be better
    events = []
    for (b, ctx) in bitctx:
        if R < 255:
            R *= 255; depth += 1
        n0, n1 = cc[ctx]
        S = R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        events.append((depth, b, S, R))
        if b:
            R -= S
            cc[ctx][1] += 1
        else:
            R = S
            cc[ctx][0] += 1
    return events, depth
```

Wait, note: the C code: the scale happens inside get_bit before split ✓. The counts are incremented after the bit ✓.

Then the cuts: for each event (depth d, bit b, S, R):
- A = (S * 255**d + R - 1) // R (i.e., ceil(S·255^d / R))
- b=0: high = min(high, A / 255^d)
- b=1: low = max(low, A / 255^d)

Optimization: I'll keep low and high as Fractions; each event does 1-2 comparisons and min/max — cheap enough. ~40k events → no problem.

Now the partitioner:

```python
import sys
data = open('/app/data.txt','rb').read()
n = len(data)

def k_for(L, v):  # the smallest k with v <= (2**k - 1) << L - 1
    # (2^k - 1)*2^L - 1 >= v
    k = 1
    while (2**k - 1) * (1 << L) - 1 < v:
        k += 1
    return k

def int_bits(L, v):
    return 2 * k_for(L, v) + L - 1
```

The greedy: I'll maintain the model counts (ctx → [n0, n1]) as I build. At position i (0-indexed, i = the number of bytes output):
- Candidates:
  - Literal: cost_estimate = the bits of the literal with the model weighting: the bits: flag (ctx1: 0), sign (ctx8: 0 for positive), the leading: ctxs 5..4+k (bits: k-1 zeros + 1 one), the trailing: ctx 891: L+k-1 = 3+k bits = the binary digits of v... I need the exact bit values: for the model weighting I need the actual bit values, not just the count. The integer coding of v: k = k_for(4, v); result_ans = v + 16; the bits: leading: k-1 zeros + 1 one; trailing: the binary digits of result_ans, excluding the leading 1: (L + k - 1) = 3 + k bits: the low 3+k bits of result_ans.
  - Copies: for offsets o ∈ some set: the match at (i - o): the longest ℓ such that data[i - o : i - o + ℓ] == data[i : i + ℓ] (ℓ ≤ n - i). For each candidate (o, ℓ): the bits: flag (ctx1: 1), offset o: k_o = k_for(5, o): leading k_o - 1 zeros + 1 one at ctxs 6..5+k_o; trailing: the low 4 + k_o bits of (o + 32) at ctx 198. Length ℓ - 1: k_l = k_for(2, ℓ - 1): leading at ctxs 3..2+k_l; trailing: the low 1 + k_l bits of (ℓ - 1 + 4) at ctx 297.
- The cost estimate: I'll simulate the bits through the model (in the correct order), accumulating Σ -log2((n_b + 1)/(n_tot + 2)) — I'll use math.log2 in float; that's fine for a ranking.
- Pick the minimum-cost candidate; update the model counts; advance.

The candidate offsets: I'll find the longest match L_max at position i: I'll use a dictionary of the 3-gram (or 4-gram) positions: for each 4-gram at i: candidate start positions: prev = i - o: I'll look for the best: the longest match = scan: I'll collect all o where data[i-4:i] == data[i-o-4:i-o]... Actually, let me reconsider the cost model's accuracy more carefully, since the budget is tight. The range coder's output for a bit sequence ≈ the sum over bits of log2(range before / range after) + an emission overhead. The range coder's byte count = the number of scales + ...: each scale emits 1 byte, and the range precision is 255/256... the total bits ≈ Σ log2(R_before/R_after) + a small constant (the final partial byte ~0-8 bits). My -log2 p model: p = (n_b + 1)/(tot + 2): this matches the interval-width ratio exactly in the real-number case: the ratio R_after/R_before = (n_1+1)/(tot+2) (for bit 1: R_after = R - S = R·(n1+1)/(...): S = R(n0+1)/(n0+n1+2): R - S = R(n1+1)/(n0+n1+2) ✓ (modulo the floor of S: the floor causes a small deviation, ≤ 1/R relative... negligible)). So Σ -log2 p ≈ the exact bit count, within ~ (a few bits × the number of events / 255^...) — very close. The byte count = ceil(total_bits_effective / 8) + ...: the encoder's interval method emits exactly the needed bytes: the number of bytes ≈ the total bits/8 + [0, ~1.5]. So my estimate is accurate within ~1-2 bytes.

```python
import sys, math
from fractions import Fraction

DATA = open('/app/data.txt','rb').read()
N = len(DATA)

# ---------- the exact C-mirroring simulation (for verification) ----------
class Sim:
    __slots__ = ('data','pos','F','R','cc')
    def __init__(self, data):
        self.data = data; self.pos = 0; self.F = 0; self.R = 1
        self.cc = [[0,0] for _ in range(2048)]  # the max ctx used is 891; 2048*2 is enough
    def gc(self):
        c = self.data[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.data) else 0xFF
        self.pos += 1
        return c
    def get_bit(self, ctx):
        if self.R < 255:
            self.R *= 255
            self.F = self.F * 255 + self.gc() - 1
        c0 = self.cc[ctx][0]; c1 = self.cc[ctx][1]
        S = self.R * (c0 + 1) // (c0 + c1 + 2)
        b = 1 if self.F >= S else 0
        if b:
            self.F -= S; self.R -= S; self.cc[ctx][1] = c1 + 1
        else:
            self.R = S; self.cc[ctx][0] = c0 + 1
        return b
    def get_integer(self, L, ctx):
        subtract_it = 1 << L
        result_ans = 1
        ctx2 = ctx * 99
        tmp = L
        while True:
            tmp += 1
            if self.get_bit(tmp + ctx2):
                break
        tmp -= 1
        for _ in range(tmp):
            result_ans = result_ans * 2 | self.get_bit(ctx2)
        return result_ans - subtract_it
    def decode(self):
        j = self.get_integer(9, 0)
        out = bytearray()
        Q = 0
        for _ in range(j):
            if self.get_bit(1):
                o = self.get_integer(5, 2)
                L = self.get_integer(2, 3) + 1
                z = Q - o - 1
                if z < 0 or z + L > Q:
                    raise RuntimeError('bad copy')
                out += out[z:z+L]
                Q += L
            else:
                sgn = self.get_bit(8)
                v = self.get_integer(4, 9)
                val = (1 - 2*sgn) * v
                out.append(val & 0xFF)
                Q += 1
        if Q != N:  # for the random test, N is passed separately
            pass
        return bytes(out)
```

The bit plan and the skeleton:

```python
def k_for(L, v):
    # the smallest k >= 1 with v <= (2**k - 1)*(1<<L) - 1
    k = 1
    while (2**k - 1) * (1 << L) - 1 < v:
        k += 1
    return k

def integer_bits(L, v, ctx):
    # returns a list of (bit, ctx) for get_integer(L, ctx)
    k = k_for(L, v)
    ans = v + (1 << L)
    out = []
    # the leading: k-1 zeros at ctxs L+1 .. L+k-1, then a 1 at ctx L+k
    for c in range(L + 1, L + k):
        out.append((0, c))
    out.append((1, L + k))
    # the trailing: the low (L + k - 1) bits of ans, MSB first, all at ctx*99
    ntr = L + k - 1
    c99 = ctx * 99
    for b in range(ntr - 1, -1, -1):
        out.append(((ans >> b) & 1, c99))
    return out
```

Verify: ans = v + 2^L ∈ [2^L, 2^(L+k) - 1]: bit length = L + k: bits L+k-1 .. 0: the top bit (position L+k-1) = 1: the trailing is ntr = L + k - 1 bits = positions L+k-2 down to 0 ✓. Total bits: (k-1) + 1 + (L+k-1) = 2k + L - 1 ✓ matches int_bits.

Verify the k boundaries: v = (2^k - 1)·2^L - 1: ans = v + 2^L = 2^L(2^k - 1) + 2^L - ... = 2^(L+k) - 2^L + 2^L - 1 = 2^(L+k) - 1: the bit length L+k ✓ fits. v = 0: ans = 2^L: the trailing: the low L bits are 0 ✓.

```python
def block_bitplan(blocks, N):
    # blocks: a list of ('C', o, L) or ('L', v)
    bits = []
    j = len(blocks)
    bits += integer_bits(9, j, 0)
    for b in blocks:
        if b[0] == 'C':
            bits.append((1, 1))
            bits += integer_bits(5, b[1], 2)
            bits += integer_bits(2, b[2] - 1, 3)
        else:
            bits.append((0, 1))
            bits.append((0, 8))   # positive
            bits += integer_bits(4, b[1], 9)
    return bits
```

The sign: for a positive v: sgn bit = 0: (1 - 2·0)·v = +v ✓.

The skeleton + the encoder:

```python
def skeleton(bits):
    R = 1; depth = 0
    cc = {}
    events = []
    for (b, ctx) in bits:
        if R < 255:
            R *= 255; depth += 1
        n0, n1 = cc.get(ctx, (0, 0))
        S = R * (n0 + 1) // (n0 + n1 + 2)
        events.append((depth, b, S, R))
        if b:
            R -= S
            cc[ctx] = (n0, n1 + 1)
        else:
            R = S
            cc[ctx] = (n0 + 1, n1)
    return events, depth

def encode(bits):
    events, depth = skeleton(bits)
    low = Fraction(0, 1); high = Fraction(1, 1)
    for (d, b, S, R) in events:
        A = (S * 255**d + R - 1) // R   # ceil(S*255^d / R)
        val = Fraction(A, 255**d)
        if b == 0:
            if val < high: high = val
        else:
            if val > low: low = val
        if low >= high:
            return None
    # pick x*
    x = (low + high) / 2
    # uniqueness check: is x of the form m/255^d?
    def safe(x):
        p, q = x.numerator, x.denominator
        while q % 3 == 0: q //= 3
        while q % 5 == 0: q //= 5
        return q != 1
    candidates = [x, (low + 2*high)/3, (2*low + high)/3, (low + high)/4 ...]
```

Hmm, more simply: if not safe(x): try a few. Actually, if low = A/255^d1, high = B/255^d2: the midpoint's denominator = 2·255^k (after reduction) → always has a factor of 2 → always safe. Prove: low = p/q1, q1 = 255^a (in lowest terms: A/255^d: after reduction the denominator divides 255^d: yes, the denominator is 255^a (a ≤ d)). Similarly high = B/255^c. Midpoint = (p·255^c + B·255^a) / (2·255^{a+c}): reduction: the denominator is 2·255^k (k ≤ a + c)... the numerator may share factors of 3, 5 with 255^{a+c}, but the factor of 2: the numerator p·255^c + B·255^a = 255^a·(p·255^{c-a} + B), if c ≥ a: the numerator is divisible by 255^a... the factor of 2: 255^a·(odd) + ...: the numerator is odd·...: p is odd? p could be even: 255^a is odd: p·255^c: the parity = the parity of p. The numerator = p·255^c + B·255^a: the parity = p + B (mod 2) (odd factors don't matter). If p + B is even: the numerator is even → the factor of 2 cancels → the denominator = 255^k: not safe! In that case the midpoint = an integer/255^k: a finite expansion: ambiguous. Fix: try (2·low + high)/3 and (low + 2·high)/3: the denominator is 3·255^k: the factor of 3 cancels?! 3·255^k: 3 = 3: the denominator after reduction: 3·3^a5^b...: the factor of 3 remains? 3·255^k = 3^{k+1}·5^k: all 3, 5s: could reduce to an integer/255^k' → not safe again. Ugh. A general fix: I'll add a denominator that has another prime: pick x = low + t·(high - low), t = 1/7: x = (6·low + high)/7: the denominator is 7·255^k → has the prime 7 ✓ always safe (7 ∤ 3·5). So x = (6·low + high)/7. And 0 ≤ x < 1 ✓. I'll use that. (t can be any rational with a denominator that has a prime other than 3, 5; I'll use 1/7.)

Now, the partitioner:

```python
def lcp(a, b, i, limit):
    # the LCP of a[i:] and a[b:]
    k = 0
    while i + k < limit and b + k < limit and a[i+k] == a[b+k]:
        k += 1
    return k
```

The cost function: I'll simulate the block's bits through the model (in the correct order), accumulating -log2 p:

```python
import math
def block_cost(block, i, model):
    if block[0] == 'L':
        bits = [(0,1), (0,8)] + integer_bits(4, block[1], 9)
    else:
        bits = [(1,1)] + integer_bits(5, block[1], 2) + integer_bits(2, block[2]-1, 3)
    cost = 0.0
    for (b, ctx) in bits:
        n0, n1 = model.get(ctx, (0,0))
        tot = n0 + n1 + 2
        p = (n0 + 1) / tot if b == 0 else (n1 + 1) / tot
        cost += -math.log2(p)
        if b == 0: model[ctx] = (n0 + 1, n1)
        else: model[ctx] = (n0, n1 + 1)
    return cost
```

Note: the model update must reflect the actual bits ✓.
